It's not like I woke up one Tuesday and decided, \"Hey, I'm going to stop brewing beer and ignore my records and just sit in my recliner.\" It just sort of... happened. I haven't done anything wrong to anybody. I'm just keeping to myself mostly. If anything, I'm just staying out of people's way. Like at work, lately I just stock my aisles, help the customers find their drywall screws or whatever, and then I take my lunch break out in my truck instead of the breakroom so I don't have to force small talk with the cashiers. So I'm not bothering a single soul. I don't feel guilty, because I'm not doing anything to feel guilty for. I just feel... flat, I guess. But no, no guilt, and I know for a fact I'm not a burden on anyone. I've always been completely independent, and that hasn't changed just because I'm in a bit of a slump right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight recently—like eating a lot more or a lot less than usual?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask about that, because I was just noticing something the other morning when I was getting dressed for work. I always wear these same heavy-duty denim jeans to the store—you know, the kind with the reinforced knees, because when you're stocking the bottom shelves in the plumbing aisle, you're down on your knees on that concrete a lot. Anyway, I've had this one pair for maybe three years, and they always fit just right, you know, snug enough that I don't really even need a belt, though I wear one anyway because it's part of the uniform code. But the other day, I pulled them on, and I had to cinch the belt up an extra notch. They were just... loose. Baggy in the seat, loose around the waist. \n\nAnd then I started thinking about it, and I realized I haven't really been eating much. It's not that I'm trying to lose weight or anything. I mean, at my age, you kind of just accept the shape you're in, right? But it's just... most days lately, I actually have to remind myself to eat. Like, I'll be at work, and my stomach might rumble a little around one o'clock, but by the time my break rolls around, I just don't have the drive to go to the breakroom fridge and heat up my Tupperware. And then when I get home... well, normally I'd fire up the stove, maybe make some pork chops or a nice big bowl of chili. I've got this great chili recipe my dad taught me, uses three different kinds of beans and a whole lot of cumin. But lately? I get home, and the thought of cooking just feels exhausting. So I'll maybe make a sandwich, or just heat up some soup, but even then, I'll sit down in my recliner with the bowl in my lap, and it just... sits there. I look at it, and it just looks like too much trouble to even lift the spoon to my mouth. It's like the appetite just isn't there at all. I might force down a few bites just because I know a guy needs fuel to keep going, but it's a chore, doctor. It really is a chore. So yeah, I guess I'm eating a lot less, and the jeans are definitely telling the story there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling restless, unable to sit still, or moving around more than usual lately?\n\nPatient: Restless? Unable to sit still? No, doc, not at all. Actually, it's pretty much the exact opposite. Like I was saying a minute ago about getting home and just sitting in my recliner with that bowl of soup... I can sit in that chair for hours without so much as twitching a muscle. It's this brown leather La-Z-Boy, got it at a discount furniture warehouse out on Route 9 about, oh, maybe five or six years ago. Best investment I ever made, honestly, especially after standing on concrete all day at the store. But anyway, I'll sit down in it after my shift, and I'll just... stay there. I don't get the urge to pace the floors or fidget or anything like that. \n\nI know what you mean, though—my sister's kid, my nephew, he’s one of those types who can't sit through a half-hour television show without bouncing his knee or getting up to look out the window or checking his phone a dozen times. It drives my sister up a wall. But me? No. I just sit there. Sometimes I don't even turn the TV on. I'll just look at the blank screen, or maybe stare out the sliding glass door to the backyard, just watching the sun go down over the fence. I don't feel like I need to be doing something or moving my hands. I used to, you know, back when I was really into my homebrewing—I'd be pacing the kitchen, checking the temperature on the boil, sterilizing the glass bottles, always moving around, always doing something with my hands. But lately? No. I'm perfectly fine just staying completely still. I don't have that jittery feeling, no nervous energy to burn off. I'm just... parked. So no, no restlessness here. Just a whole lot of sitting.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that your mood is extremely low, almost as if you're emotionally numb, and that nothing could cheer you up—not even things that usually make you happy?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, that’s exactly it. Emotionally numb is a good way to put it. Because, see, a little while back, I had this real big burst of energy. I mean, I was going a mile a minute, getting all sorts of projects done around the house, feeling like I could take on the world. But then... well, after that burst of energy wore off, it was like night and day, doc. I felt exactly like somebody had just reached over and pulled the plug right out of the wall on me. Just a total power failure. \n\nI have this little square kitchen table, just a cheap laminate thing I picked up at a yard sale years ago, sits right by the window looking out at the side yard. And I’d get up in the morning before my shift at the store, and I would just sit at that table staring at my coffee. I wouldn't even drink it. I use this old Stanley Tools mug that a vendor gave me back in the nineties, and I'd pour my coffee—I take it black, always have—and I'd just sit there watching the steam stop rising until it was ice cold. And sitting there, I just felt so... empty. Completely hollowed out inside. And ashamed, too. I don't even fully know why, but I'd just sit there staring at my coffee feeling empty and ashamed that I couldn't muster up the will to even take a sip, let alone face the day. \n\nAnd you asked about things that usually cheer me up... it’s like they don't even exist anymore. I told you about my records, right? I've got a great vinyl collection. Usually, if I'm feeling a little down, I can put on some Bob Seger or maybe The Eagles, and it just sets me right. It lifts the spirits. But when this... this plug gets pulled, I could have my favorite album playing right in my ear and I wouldn't feel a single thing. It's just noise. Nothing touches it. It’s just that heavy, flat, empty feeling, and it doesn't budge no matter what I try to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any other changes or symptoms along with this low mood—like trouble concentrating, making decisions, or feelings of worthlessness?\n\nPatient: Trouble concentrating? No, I wouldn't say that at all. Actually, my focus is still pretty sharp, surprisingly enough. Just this past Tuesday, my manager, Gary—he's a good guy, but a little disorganized, honestly—he had me doing the quarterly inventory on the brass plumbing fittings. And I don't know if you've ever spent much time in a hardware store, doc, but do you know how many different types of brass fittings there are? You've got your elbows, your tees, your reducers, your couplers, and they all look practically identical if you aren't paying close attention. A half-inch threaded elbow looks an awful lot like a five-eighths, especially since the fluorescent lights in aisle four have been flickering for a month and nobody's bothered to change the bulbs. But I went through four whole bins, counting every single piece, tallying it all up on the clipboard. Didn't mess up once, didn't lose my place. So my mind is still working just fine. I can focus on a task without my mind wandering off. \n\nAnd making decisions... well, that hasn't really been an issue either. I mean, I don't have to make huge, life-altering choices every day, but if a customer comes in asking whether they should use PVC or PEX tubing for a bathroom remodel, I can walk them through the pros and cons and make a call on what they need right then and there. Or even just everyday things, like deciding what route to drive to work to avoid the construction on Main Street. They're tearing up the asphalt down by the old post office again, which is the second time this year, and it backs up traffic for a mile. So I just decided to take the back roads by the river. I didn't have to sit there agonizing over it, I just made the choice and went. \n\nAnd as for feeling worthless... no, I wouldn't use that word. Like I was saying before, I feel empty, and I feel ashamed when I'm just sitting there staring at my coffee because I can't muster up the energy to move. But worthless? No. I know my value. I've been at the store for almost fifteen years now, and I know more about the inventory than half the management team combined. I know I'm a good worker, I know I'm a decent person, and I know I matter to my sister and my nephew. It's more like... well, it's like a car that's completely out of gas. The engine won't turn over, and it's just sitting there in the driveway doing nothing. But the car itself isn't a worthless piece of junk, you know? It just doesn't have any fuel in it right now. So no, my brain is working, I can make a choice, and I know I'm not useless. It's really just that heavy, flat, empty feeling I told you about.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you’ve had days where you felt your mood was extremely low, you lost interest in most things, and you also had a noticeable change in your appetite, all at the same time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they definitely all hit exactly at the same time. It’s like a package deal. When I get in that state I was telling you about—where the plug is pulled and I’m just staring at my cold coffee, feeling totally empty and not caring about my records or anything else—the food just goes right out the window with it. \n\nI mean, I'm a guy who usually appreciates a decent meal. I’ve got this old slow cooker, one of those heavy ceramic ones from the eighties, and I used to love throwing a chuck roast in there with some baby carrots and yellow potatoes before I left for the store. I'd come home and the whole house would smell great, ready to go. But lately? When I'm feeling this low? Most days I actually have to remind myself to eat. I'll be sitting in that brown La-Z-Boy I mentioned, and I'll look up at the clock on the wall—it's this old analog clock shaped like a hubcap my buddy gave me years ago—and I'll realize it's eight or nine o'clock at night and I haven't had a single bite of solid food all day. \n\nAnd even when I do realize I should probably put something in my stomach, it doesn't really help. I'll go into the kitchen and maybe get something out for supper. Nothing fancy, maybe just a can of soup or a couple slices of ham for a sandwich, but... it just sits there on the counter. I'll look at it, and honestly, doc, it just looks like too much trouble. The whole process of putting it on a plate, chewing it, swallowing it... it just feels exhausting. I don't have the desire for it at all. So I just leave it sitting there and go back to my chair. \n\nAnd it's definitely starting to show on me. I've noticed it a lot at work lately. We have a strict dress code at the hardware store—we have to wear these standard blue denim jeans along with our red company vests, they like us all looking uniform for the customers—and my jeans are noticeably looser. I've had to pull my leather belt an extra notch tighter the past couple of weeks just to keep my pants from sagging while I'm walking down the plumbing aisle. So, yeah, when that heavy, empty feeling comes around, my appetite just completely packs its bags and leaves.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt this same kind of low mood for at least two weeks, where you lost interest in things and felt emotionally numb?\n\nPatient: Before this? Well... let me think on that. I mean, sure, I’ve had times in my life where I felt a little down, just like anybody else. You know, like when my old dog Buster passed away back in 2018—he was a golden retriever mix, just a great dog, had him for eleven years—and I was pretty heartbroken for a week or two after that. But nothing like this. This is a whole different animal entirely. \n\nBecause, see, this current low spell... it’s been hanging on for about three months now since that crash I told you about. Yeah, right around three solid months. I remember it specifically because the crash happened right around the end of the big mid-summer inventory push we do at the store. Like I said before, I was running on all cylinders, getting all sorts of projects done around the house. I even rebuilt the carburetor on my push mower that same weekend, just humming along. But then the bottom just completely dropped out. \n\nAnd ever since then, for three whole months, it's just been this relentless... nothingness. Day in, day out. You wake up hoping today is the day the power comes back on, that maybe you'll actually want to put on a Bob Seger record or eat a hot meal, but it's just... nope. Three solid months of sitting in that brown La-Z-Boy, staring at the wall, or looking at that cold cup of coffee. \n\nSo, to answer your question, no, I've never had anything drag on like this before in my entire life. I've never felt this emotionally numb before this happened. Three months is a mighty long time to feel like a car with no gas, doc. It really makes you wonder if the engine's ever gonna turn over again, you know? You just keep waiting for a spark, but it's been ninety days of this heavy, flat feeling, and... well, here we are.\n\nDoctor: Before this current episode, have you ever had three or more separate periods in your life when you felt significantly down or lost interest in things for at least a couple of weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Three or more separate times? No, no, definitely not. Like I was just saying a minute ago, this whole thing is completely new territory for me. I mean, you really don't stay at the same hardware store for almost fifteen years if you're dropping off the map for weeks at a time every few years, right? \n\nMy manager, Gary—I mentioned him earlier, the guy who had me counting all those brass plumbing fittings—he's been my boss for the last eight of those years. And before that, I worked over at the old lumber yard out on Route 9, the one that got bought out by that big corporate chain back around '08 or '09. I was there for a solid decade. And in all that time, through both of those jobs, I was always the guy who showed up. Rain or shine, sleet or snow. I'd punch the clock at 6:45 AM, grab my cup from the breakroom—back at the lumber yard we had one of those old metal percolators that made the coffee taste a little bit like burnt pennies, but you drank it anyway just to thaw your hands out in the winter—and I'd be right out in the yard stacking two-by-fours or helping contractors load up their flatbeds all day. I never missed a beat. \n\nSo, if you're asking if I've had three, or even two, separate periods in my life where I just lost interest in my records or my meals for weeks on end... no. Absolutely not. I mean, sure, maybe a grumpy weekend here and there if I caught a bad summer cold or if the transmission was acting up on my truck, but nothing that lasted for weeks. I've never been the type to just lose my spark over and over again. This whole empty, out-of-gas feeling that's been dragging on for the last three months... this is the very first time I've ever dealt with anything even remotely like this.\n\nDoctor: Before this current episode, have you ever had a period lasting two weeks or more when you felt down or lost interest in most things, even if it didn’t last as long as this time?\n\nPatient: Doc, I feel like we’re going in circles a little bit here. Like I was just saying, no. Never before this current stretch. I’ve never had a two-week period, or a month, or anything like that in the past where I felt this way. It really is just this one single time, starting three months ago. \n\nI know I already mentioned the coffee and the plug being pulled earlier, but that’s really the only way I know how to explain it to you. After that huge burst of energy I had finally wore off—you know, when I was buzzing around rebuilding that carburetor and getting all those chores done—I just crashed. I felt like somebody had literally pulled the plug on me. Just yanked the cord right out of the wall. \n\nI would just sit there at my kitchen table—it’s this old, yellow Formica table I picked up at a neighborhood garage sale way back in '98, it’s got a little chip on the corner but it’s real sturdy with those chrome legs—and I would just sit there for hours. I'd be staring at my coffee, watching the steam stop rising, feeling completely empty. \n\nAnd the part I didn't tell you earlier is that I felt so ashamed, too. Just incredibly ashamed. I mean, I’m a fifty-four-year-old man who has worked hard his whole life, right? I pride myself on being reliable. So to just sit there like a lump, completely hollowed out, unable to even muster the will to drink a cup of Folgers or put on a record... it makes a man feel pretty pathetic. You just sit there judging yourself, wondering why you can't just snap out of it and be normal. But to answer your question again, doc, no. Before this whole crash happened, I never experienced anything like this in my life.\n\nDoctor: Before this current episode, have you ever had three or more separate times in your life when you felt down or lost interest in most things for at least two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Doc, I... I really don't mean to be disrespectful, but I feel like I'm taking crazy pills here. Are you... are you writing down what I'm saying? Because I feel like I just answered this exact same question. Like, literally two minutes ago. \n\nI know you doctors have your checklists and your clipboards and you have to go down the line and ask everything in a certain order, but... no. No, I have never had three or more separate times in my life where I felt this way. I haven't even had *two* times. I haven't had *one* time before this current stretch. \n\nLike I said about my jobs—the lumber yard, the hardware store—I've always been the guy who shows up. I don't have a history of just... checking out on life for weeks at a time. I've always been steady. Steady as a rock, my dad used to say. He was a machinist, worked at the same plant for thirty-five years, and he always told me, \"You show up, you do the work, you don't complain.\" And that's how I've lived my life. Up until three months ago when the bottom fell out. \n\nSo, I don't know how else to say it, doc. I really don't. I haven't had three times, or two times, or anything like that. Just this one, single, miserable three-month stretch that I'm in right now. I hope that clears it up, because I feel like I'm just repeating myself over and over."
